G.3
Proof of
Theorem

5

Proof of
Theorem

5
.

We first define
r_{p}(\|{\bm{\epsilon}}\|_{2})=p({\bm{\epsilon}})
and
r_{q}(\|{\bm{\epsilon}}\|_{2})=q({\bm{\epsilon}})
, then easily seen the concrete expressions of
r_{p}
and
r_{q}
are:

\displaystyle r_{p}(t)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{(2\sigma^{\prime 2})^{d/2-k}\pi^{d/2}}\cdot\dfrac{\Gamma(d/2)}{\Gamma(d/2-k)},

(82)

\displaystyle r_{q}(t)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\nu}{(2\sigma^{\prime 2})^{d/2-k}\pi^{d/2}}\cdot\dfrac{\Gamma(d/2)}{\Gamma(d/2-k)},

(83)

where

\nu:=\dfrac{\Gamma(d/2-k)}{\gamma(d/2-k,\frac{T^{2}}{2\sigma^{\prime 2}})}>1

(84)

and
\gamma
is the lower incomplete Gamma function.

Now we use level-set integration similar as the proof in
Lemma

F.3
to get the expressions of
P
,
Q
, and
R
respectively.
Since
{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}
are
\ell_{2}
-symmetric, without loss of generality, we let
{\bm{\delta}}=(r,0,\dots,0)^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}
.

(P)
.

Suppose
P_{T}=r_{p}(T)
.

\displaystyle P(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pr_{{\bm{\epsilon}}\sim{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{N}}^{\mathsf{g}}(k,\sigma)}[p({\bm{\epsilon}}-{\bm{\delta}})<\lambda_{1}p({\bm{\epsilon}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{\epsilon}})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\mathbb{I}}[p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}})]p({\bm{x}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{P_{T}}y\mathrm{d}y\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}p({\bm{x}})=y\\
p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}})\end{subarray}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}}{\|\nabla p({\bm{x}})\|_{2}}+

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\int_{P_{T}}^{\infty}y\mathrm{d}y\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}p({\bm{x}})=y\\
p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\nu)p({\bm{x}})\end{subarray}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}}{\|\nabla p({\bm{x}})\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{P_{T}}y\mathrm{d}y\dfrac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}r_{p}^{-1}(y)^{d-1}\left(-\dfrac{1}{r_{p}^{\prime}(r_{p}^{-1}(y))}\right)\cdot

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\Pr[p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})\leq\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}})\,|\,p({\bm{x}})=y]+

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\int_{P_{T}}^{\infty}y\mathrm{d}y\dfrac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}r_{p}^{-1}(y)^{d-1}\left(-\dfrac{1}{r_{p}^{\prime}(r_{p}^{-1}(y))}\right)\cdot

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\Pr[p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\nu)p({\bm{x}})\,|\,p({\bm{x}})=y]

\displaystyle\overset{y=r_{p}(t)}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{T}^{\infty}r_{p}(t)\mathrm{d}t\dfrac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}t^{d-1}\cdot

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\Pr[p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}})\,|\,\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=t]+

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\int_{0}^{T}r_{p}(t)\mathrm{d}t\dfrac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}t^{d-1}\cdot

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\Pr[p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\nu)p({\bm{x}})\,|\,\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{\Gamma(d/2-k)}\int_{T^{2}/(2\sigma^{\prime 2})}^{\infty}t^{d/2-k-1}\exp(-t)\mathrm{d}t\cdot

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\Pr[p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}})\,|\,\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}]+

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\dfrac{1}{\Gamma(d/2-k)}\int_{0}^{T^{2}/(2\sigma^{\prime 2})}t^{d/2-k-1}\exp(-t)\mathrm{d}t\cdot

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\Pr[p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\nu)p({\bm{x}})\,|\,\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}u_{3}(t,\lambda_{1}),&t\geq T^{2}/(2\sigma^{\prime 2})\\
u_{3}(t,\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\nu).&t<T^{2}/(2\sigma^{\prime 2})\end{array}\right.

Here,
u_{3}(t,\lambda)=\Pr[p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<\lambda p({\bm{x}})\,|\,\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}]
.

(Q)
.

Similarly,

\displaystyle Q(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pr_{{\bm{\epsilon}}\sim{\mathcal{Q}}={\mathcal{N}}^{\mathsf{g}}_{\mathsf{trunc}}(k,T,\sigma)}[p({\bm{\epsilon}}-{\bm{\delta}})<\lambda_{1}p({\bm{\epsilon}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{\epsilon}})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}\leq T}{\mathbb{I}}[p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}})]q({\bm{x}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\nu P_{T}}^{\infty}y\mathrm{d}y\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}q({\bm{x}})=y\\
p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\nu)p({\bm{x}})\end{subarray}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}}{\|\nabla q({\bm{x}})\|_{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{y=r_{q}(t)}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}r_{q}(t)\mathrm{d}t\dfrac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}t^{d-1}\cdot

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\Pr[p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\nu)p({\bm{x}})\,|\,\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{3}(t,\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\nu)\cdot{\mathbb{I}}\left[t\leq\frac{T^{2}}{2\sigma^{\prime 2}}\right].

(R)
.

Now, for
R
:

\displaystyle R(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pr_{{\bm{\epsilon}}\sim{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{N}}^{\mathsf{g}}(k,\sigma)}[p({\bm{\epsilon}})<\lambda_{1}p({\bm{\epsilon}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{\epsilon}}+{\bm{\delta}})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\mathbb{I}}[p({\bm{x}})<\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})]p({\bm{x}})\mathrm{d}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{4}(t,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}).

Here,
u_{4}(t,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\Pr[\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})\,|\,\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}]
.

Plugging
Lemma

G.1
into
P(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
and
Q(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, and then plugging
Lemma

G.2
into
R(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, we yield the desired expressions in theorem statement.
∎

Lemma G.1
.

Under the condition of
Theorem

5
,
let
{\bm{\delta}}=(r,0,\dots,0)^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}
,

\displaystyle u_{3}(t,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\Pr[p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<\lambda p({\bm{x}})\,|\,\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}]

(85)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\dfrac{(r+\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})^{2}}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}-\dfrac{2k\sigma^{\prime 2}W(\frac{t}{k}e^{\frac{t}{k}}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{k}})}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}\right),

where
W
is the principal branch of Lambert
W
function.

Proof of
Lemma

G.1
.

p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<\lambda p({\bm{x}})
means that

r_{p}(\|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}}\|_{2})<\lambda r_{p}(\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2})
and therefore

\|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}}\|_{2}>r_{p}^{-1}(\lambda r_{p}(\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}))
.
Given that
\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}
,
we have

\left\{\begin{aligned} &x_{1}^{2}+\sum_{i=2}^{d}x_{i}^{2}=2t\sigma^{\prime 2},\\
&(x_{1}-r)^{2}+\sum_{i=2}^{d}x_{i}^{2}\geq r_{p}^{-1}(\lambda r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}))^{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

(86)

This is equivalent to

x_{1}\leq\dfrac{2t\sigma^{\prime 2}+r^{2}-r_{p}^{-1}(\lambda r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}))^{2}}{2r}.

(87)

From the expression of
r_{p}
(
Equation

82
),
we have

r_{p}^{-1}(\lambda r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}))^{2}=2\sigma^{\prime 2}kW\left(\frac{t}{k}e^{\frac{t}{k}}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{k}}\right).

(88)

Thus,
when
\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}
and
{\bm{x}}
uniformly sampled from this sphere,

\displaystyle p({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\delta}})<\lambda p({\bm{x}})

(89)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle x_{1}\leq\dfrac{2t\sigma^{\prime 2}+r^{2}-2\sigma^{\prime 2}kW(\frac{t}{k}e^{\frac{t}{k}}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{k}})}{2r}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\dfrac{1+\frac{x_{1}}{\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}}{2}\leq\dfrac{(r+\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})^{2}-2\sigma^{\prime 2}kW\left(\frac{t}{k}e^{\frac{t}{k}}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{k}}\right)}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}.

According to
(
Yang et al. 2020
, Lemma I.23)
, for
{\bm{x}}
uniformly sampled from sphere with radius
\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}
, the component coordinate
\dfrac{1+\frac{x_{1}}{\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}}{2}\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\frac{d-1}{2},\frac{d-1}{2})
.
Combining
Equation

89
with this result concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma G.2
.

Under the condition of
Theorem

5
,
let
{\bm{\delta}}=(r,0,\dots,0)^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}
,

\displaystyle u_{4}(t,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle:=\Pr[\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})

(90)

\displaystyle|\,\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}u_{1}(t),&\lambda_{1}\leq 0\\
u_{1}(t)+u_{2}(t),&\lambda_{1}>0\end{array}\right.

where

\displaystyle u_{1}(t)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\dfrac{\min\{T^{2},2\sigma^{\prime 2}kW(\frac{t}{k}e^{\frac{t}{k}}(\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2})^{\frac{1}{k}})\}}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\dfrac{(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}-r)^{2}}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}\right),

\displaystyle u_{2}(t)=

\displaystyle\max\left\{0,\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\dfrac{2\sigma^{\prime 2}kW(\frac{t}{k}e^{\frac{t}{k}}\lambda_{1}^{\frac{1}{k}})-(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}-r)^{2}}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\dfrac{T^{2}-(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}-r)^{2}}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}\right)\right\},

Proof of
Lemma

G.2
.

Under the condition that
\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}
, we separate two cases:
q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>0
and
q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})=0
, which corresponds to
\|{\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}}\|_{2}\leq T
and
\|{\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}}\|_{2}>T
.

(1)

q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>0
:

Notice that

\displaystyle q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>0

(91)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\|{\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq T^{2}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle x_{1}\leq\dfrac{T^{2}-2t\sigma^{\prime 2}-r^{2}}{2r}.

From
Equation

83
,
q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})=\nu p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})

(92)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle(\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2})p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})\geq r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\|{\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq r_{p}^{-1}\left(\dfrac{r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})}{\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle x_{1}\leq\dfrac{r_{p}^{-1}\left(\frac{r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})}{\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2}}\right)^{2}-2t\sigma^{\prime 2}-r^{2}}{2r}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Pr[\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})\wedge q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>0\,|\ \|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}]

(93)

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[x_{1}\leq\dfrac{\min\left\{r_{p}^{-1}\left(\frac{r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})}{\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2}}\right)^{2},T^{2}\right\}-2t\sigma^{\prime 2}-r^{2}}{2r}\,\Big|\,\|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}\right].

By
(
Yang et al. 2020
, Lemma I.23)
and
Equation

88
, we thus have

\displaystyle\Pr[\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})

(94)

\displaystyle\wedge q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>0\,|\ \|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\dfrac{\min\{T^{2},2\sigma^{\prime 2}kW(\frac{t}{k}e^{\frac{t}{k}}(\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2})^{\frac{1}{k}})\}}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\dfrac{(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}-r)^{2}}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}\right)=u_{1}(t).

(2)

q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})=0
:

Similarly,

q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})=0\iff x_{1}>\dfrac{T^{2}-2t\sigma^{\prime 2}-r^{2}}{2r}.

(95)

When
\lambda_{1}\leq 0
, the condition
\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})=\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})
can never be satisfied.
When
\lambda_{1}>0
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})

(96)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\|{\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq r_{p}^{-1}\left(\dfrac{r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})}{\lambda_{1}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle x_{1}\leq\dfrac{r_{p}^{-1}\left(\frac{r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})}{\lambda_{1}}\right)^{2}-2t\sigma^{\prime 2}-r^{2}}{2r}.

Therefore, when
\lambda_{1}\leq 0
,

\displaystyle\Pr[\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})

(97)

\displaystyle\wedge q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})=0\,|\ \|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}]=0.

When
\lambda_{1}>0
, the condition that
lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})
is equivalent to

x_{1}\in\left(\dfrac{T^{2}-2t\sigma^{\prime 2}-r^{2}}{2r},\dfrac{r_{p}^{-1}\left(\frac{r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})}{\lambda_{1}}\right)^{2}-2t\sigma^{\prime 2}-r^{2}}{2r}\right].

(98)

Again, by
(
Yang et al. 2020
, Lemma I.23)
and
Equation

88
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})

(99)

\displaystyle\wedge q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})=0\,|\ \|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max\left\{0,\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\dfrac{2\sigma^{\prime 2}kW(\frac{t}{k}e^{\frac{t}{k}}\lambda_{1}^{\frac{1}{k}})-(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}-r)^{2}}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\dfrac{T^{2}-(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}-r)^{2}}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u_{2}(t).

(3)
Combining the two cases:

Now we are ready to combine the two cases.

\displaystyle\Pr[\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})

(100)

\displaystyle|\ \|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pr[\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})

\displaystyle\wedge q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>0\,|\ \|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}]+

\displaystyle\Pr[\lambda_{1}p({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})>r_{p}(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t})

\displaystyle\wedge q({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\delta}})=0\,|\ \|{\bm{x}}\|_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}u_{1}(t),&\lambda_{1}\leq 0\\
u_{1}(t)+u_{2}(t).&\lambda_{1}>0\end{array}\right.

∎

G.4
Discussion on Uniqueness of Feasible Pair

As we sketched in
Section

5.3
, in general cases, the pair that satisfies constraints in
Equation

12
is unique.
We formally state this finding and prove it in
Theorem

7
.

Theorem 7

(Uniqueness of Feasible Solution in
Equation

12
)
.

Under the same setting of
Theorem

5
, if
Q_{A}\in(0,1)
and
P_{A}\in(Q_{A}/\nu,1-(1-Q_{A})/\nu)
, then there is the pair
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
that satisfies both
P(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=P_{A}
and
Q(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=Q_{A}
is unique.

We prove the theorem in the end of this section, which is based on the strict monotonicity of two auxiliary functions:
g(\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2}):=Q(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
and
h(\lambda_{1})
(defined in
Section

5.3
).
For other types of smoothing distributions
{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}
, in
Theorem

11
we characterize and prove a sufficient condition that guarantees the uniqueness of feasible pair.

We observe that the feasible region of
(P_{A},Q_{A})
is

{\mathcal{R}}=\{(x,y):y/\nu\leq x\leq 1-(1-y)/\nu,0\leq y\leq 1\}.

(101)

Therefore,
the theorem states that when
(P_{A},Q_{A})
is an internal point of
{\mathcal{R}}
, the feasible solution is unique and we can use our proposed method to find out such a feasible solution and thus solve the dual problem
({\bm{\mathsfit{D}}})
.
Now, the edge cases are that
(P_{A},Q_{A})
lies on the boundary of
{\mathcal{R}}
.
We discuss all these cases and show that these boundary cases correspond to degenerate problems that are easy to solve respectively:

•

Q_{A}=0
:

When
Q_{A}=0
, and
P_{A}\in(0,1)
(otherwise, trivially
R(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=P_{A}\in\{0,1\}
solves
({\bm{\mathsfit{D}}})
), we have

\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2}\to 0^{+}
and thus
R(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{2}(t)
.
Since
u_{2}(t)
only involves
\lambda_{1}
, we only require
\lambda_{1}
to be unique to deploy the method.
Since
P_{A}=P(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{3}(t,\lambda_{1})\cdot{\mathbb{I}}[t\geq T^{2}/(2\sigma^{\prime 2})]
and
P_{A}\in(0,1)
, by similar arguments as in
Theorem

7
, we know
\lambda_{1}
is unique.
Hence, all feasible pairs give the same
R(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, i.e., have the same objective value and the proposed method that computes a feasible one is sufficient for solving
({\bm{\mathsfit{D}}})
.

•

Q_{A}=1
:

When
Q_{A}=1
and
P_{A}\in(0,1)
, we observe that
u_{1}(t)\leq\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\frac{T^{2}}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}-\frac{(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}-r)^{2}}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}\right)
where equality is feasible with the selected
(\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2})\to+\infty
and hence the maximum of
\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{1}(t)
among all feasible
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
is a constant.
On the other hand, since
u_{2}(t)
only involves
\lambda_{1}
that is unique as discussed in “
Q_{A}=0
” case, all feasible pairs give the same value of
\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{2}(t)
.
As a result, the maximum of
R(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
can be computed by adding the unique value of
\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{2}(t)
and the constant corresponding to the maximum of
\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{1}(t)
among all feasible
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, which solves the dual problem
({\bm{\mathsfit{D}}})
.

•

P_{A}=Q_{A}/\nu
:

We assume
P_{A},Q_{A}\in(0,1)
(otherwise covered by former cases).
In this case,
\lambda_{1}
satisfies that
h(\lambda_{1})=P_{A}-Q_{A}/\nu=0
, so
\lambda_{1}\to 0^{+}
.
As a result,
u_{2}(t)=0
for all
t>0
and
R(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{1}(t)
.
We observe that
u_{1}(t)
is only related to
(\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2})
where
(\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2})
satisfying
Q(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=Q_{A}
is unique since
Q_{A}\in(0,1)
.
Thus, any feasible
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
would have the same
(\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2})
and hence leads to the same
R(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
.
So the proposed method that finds one feasible
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
suffices for solving
({\bm{\mathsfit{D}}})
.

•

P_{A}=1-\frac{1-Q_{A}}{\nu}
:

We again assume
P_{A},Q_{A}\in(0,1)
(otherwise covered by former cases).
In this case,
\lambda_{1}
satisfies that
h(\lambda_{1})=1-1/\nu
.
Since
\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}{\mathbb{I}}[t<T^{2}/(2\sigma^{\prime 2})]=1/\nu
, we know
u_{3}(t,\lambda_{1})=1
for
t\geq T^{2}/(2\sigma^{\prime 2})
except a zero-measure set, and thus
\lambda_{1}\to+\infty
.
As a result,
\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{2}(t)=1-\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\frac{T^{2}-(\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}-r)^{2}}{4r\sigma^{\prime}\sqrt{2t}}\right)
is a constant.
Similar as “
P_{A}=Q_{A}/\nu
” case, feasible
(\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2})
is unique.
Therefore, feasible
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
leads to a unique
\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{1}(t)
.
We compute out these two quantities
\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{2}(t)
and
\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{1}(t)
so as to obtain the unique
R(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
that solves
({\bm{\mathsfit{D}}})
.

We remark that in practice, we never observe any instances that correspond to these edge cases though we implemented these techniques for solving them.

Proof of
Theorem

7
.

The high-level proof sketch is implied in the derivation of our feasible
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
finding method introduced in
Section

5.3
.
We first show
h(\lambda_{1})
is monotonically strictly increasing in a neighborhood of
\lambda_{1}
where
h(\lambda_{1})=P_{A}-Q_{A}/\nu
, so the
\lambda_{1}
that satisfies
P_{A}-Q_{A}/\nu
is unique.
We then define
g(\gamma)=\nu\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2-k,1)}u_{3}(t,\gamma)\cdot{\mathbb{I}}[t\leq T^{2}/(2\sigma^{\prime 2})]
, and show its strict monotonicity around the neighborhood of
\lambda^{*}
that satisfies
g(\gamma^{*})=Q_{A}
, which indicates that
(\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2})
that satisfies
Q_{A}
is unique.
Combining this two arguments, we know feasible
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
is unique.
Note that the key in this proof is the local
strict
monotonicity, and the global (nonstrict) monotonicity for both
h(\cdot)
and
g(\cdot)
is apparent from their expressions.
We now present the proofs for the local strict monotonicity of these two functions
h
and
g
.

(1)
h(\lambda_{1})
is monotonically strictly increasing in a neighborhood of
\lambda_{1}
where
h(\lambda_{1})=P_{A}-Q_{A}/\nu
.

From the theorem condition, we know that
h(\lambda_{1})\in(0,1-1/\nu)
.
Thus, there exists
t_{0}\geq T^{2}/(2\sigma^{\prime 2})
, such that
u_{3}(t_{0},\lambda_{1})\in(0,1)
(otherwise
h(\lambda_{1})=0\text{ or }1-1/\nu
).
From the closed-form equation of
u_{3}
, for any neighboring
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\neq\lambda_{1}
, we will have
W(t_{0}/ke^{t_{0}/k}\lambda_{1}^{-1/k})\neq W(t_{0}/ke^{t_{0}/k}\lambda_{1}^{\prime-1/k})
by the monotonicity of Lambert W function.
On the other hand, since
u_{3}(t_{0},\lambda_{1})\in(0,1)
and
\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\cdot)
is also strict monotonic in the neighborhood, we have
u_{3}(t_{0},\lambda_{1}^{\prime})\neq u_{3}(t_{0},\lambda_{1})
.
Same happens to
t_{0}
’s neighborhood, i.e.,
\exists\delta>0
, s.t.,
\forall t\in(t_{0}-\delta,t_{0}+\delta)
,
u_{3}(t,\lambda_{1}^{\prime})\neq u_{3}(t,\lambda_{1})
and
\mathrm{sgn}(u_{3}(t,\lambda_{1}^{\prime})-u_{3}(t,\lambda_{1}))
is consistent for any
t\in(t_{0}-\delta,t_{0}+\delta)
.
As a result,
h(\lambda_{1})\neq h(\lambda_{1}^{\prime})
. By definition and the fact that
h(\cdot)
is monotonically non-decreasing, the argument is proved.

(2)
g(\gamma)
is monotonically strictly increasing in a neighborhood of
\gamma^{*}
where
g(\gamma^{*})=Q_{A}
.

Since
Q_{A}\in(0,1)
by the theorem condition, we know that there exists
t_{0}\in(0,T^{2}/(2\sigma^{\prime 2}))
, such that
u_{3}(t_{0},\gamma^{*})\in(0,1)
(otherwise
g(\gamma^{*})=0\text{ or }1
, which contradicts the theorem condition).
Following the same reasoning as in (1)’s proof, for any
\gamma^{\prime}\neq\gamma^{*}
that lies in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
\gamma^{*}
, we have
u_{3}(t_{0},\gamma^{*})\neq u_{3}(t_{0},\gamma^{\prime})
, and
\exists\delta>0
, s.t.,
\forall t\in(t_{0}-\delta,t_{0}+\delta)
,
u_{3}(t,\gamma^{*})\neq u_{3}(t,\gamma^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{sgn}(u_{3}(t,\gamma^{*})-u_{3}(t,\gamma^{\prime}))
is consistent for any
t\in(t_{0}-\delta,t_{0}+\delta)
.
As a result,
g(\gamma^{*})\neq g(\gamma^{\prime})
.
By definition and the fact that
g(\cdot)
is monotonically non-decreasing, the argument is proved.
∎

Appendix H
Extensions of DSRS Computational Methods

In this appendix, we exemplify a few extensions of DSRS computational method.

H.1
Certification with Standard and Truncated Standard Gaussian

In main text and
Theorem

5
, we focus on DSRS certification with generalized Gaussian as
{\mathcal{P}}
and truncated generalized Gaussian as
{\mathcal{Q}}
, which has theoretical advantages (
Theorem

2
).
On the other hand, DSRS can also be applied to other distributions.
Concretely, to certify robustness with standard Gaussian as
{\mathcal{P}}
and truncated standard Gaussian as
{\mathcal{Q}}
, we can directly plug the following theorem’s numerical integration expressions into the described DSRS algorithm (
Algorithm

2
).

Theorem 8
.

In
{\bm{\mathsfit{D}}}_{{\bm{\delta}}}(P_{A},Q_{A})
, let
r=\|{\bm{\delta}}\|_{2}
,
when
{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{N}}(\sigma)
and
{\mathcal{Q}}={\mathcal{N}}_{\mathsf{trunc}}(T,\sigma)
,
let
\nu:=\Gamma\mathrm{CDF}_{d/2}(T^{2}/(2\sigma^{2}))^{-1}
,

\displaystyle R(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}):=

\displaystyle\Pr_{{\bm{\epsilon}}\sim{\mathcal{P}}}[p({\bm{\epsilon}})<\lambda_{1}p({\bm{\epsilon}}+{\bm{\delta}})+\lambda_{2}q(v\epsilon+{\bm{\delta}})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2,1)}u_{1}(t),&\lambda_{1}\leq 0\\
\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2,1)}u_{1}(t)+u_{2}(t),&\lambda_{1}>0\end{array}\right.\mathrm{where}

\displaystyle u_{1}(t)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\dfrac{\min\{T^{2},2t\sigma^{2}+2\sigma^{2}\ln(\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2})\}}{4r\sigma\sqrt{2t}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\dfrac{(\sigma\sqrt{2t}-r)^{2}}{4r\sigma\sqrt{2t}}\right),

\displaystyle u_{2}(t)=

\displaystyle\max\left\{0,\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\dfrac{2t\sigma^{2}+2\sigma^{2}\ln\lambda_{1}-(\sigma\sqrt{2t}-r)^{2}}{4r\sigma\sqrt{2t}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\dfrac{T^{2}-(\sigma\sqrt{2t}-r)^{2}}{4r\sigma\sqrt{2t}}\right)\right\}.

\displaystyle P(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle:=\Pr_{{\bm{\epsilon}}\sim{\mathcal{P}}}[p({\bm{\epsilon}}-{\bm{\delta}})<\lambda_{1}p({\bm{\epsilon}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{\epsilon}})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2,1)}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}u_{3}(t,\lambda_{1}),&t\geq T^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})\\
u_{3}(t,\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2}),&t<T^{2}/(2\sigma^{2}).\end{array}\right.\mathrm{where}

\displaystyle u_{3}(t,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\dfrac{1}{2}+\dfrac{r^{2}+2\sigma^{2}\ln\lambda}{4r\sigma\sqrt{2t}}\right).

\displaystyle Q(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle:=\Pr_{{\bm{\epsilon}}\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}[p({\bm{\epsilon}}-{\bm{\delta}})<\lambda_{1}p({\bm{\epsilon}})+\lambda_{2}q({\bm{\epsilon}})]

\displaystyle=\nu\mathbb{E}_{t\sim\Gamma(d/2,1)}u_{3}(t,\lambda_{1}+\nu\lambda_{2})\cdot{\mathbb{I}}[t\leq T^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})].

In above equations,
\Gamma(d/2,1)
is gamma distribution and
\Gamma\mathrm{CDF}_{d/2}
is its CDF, and
\mathrm{BetaCDF}_{\frac{d-1}{2}}
is the CDF of distribution
\mathrm{Beta}(\frac{d-1}{2},\frac{d-1}{2})
.

Proof of
Theorem

8
.

The proof largely follows the same procedure as the proof of
Theorem

5
.
The only difference is that, since
{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{N}}(\sigma)
, let
r_{p}(\|{\bm{\epsilon}}\|_{2})=p({\bm{\epsilon}})
, different from
Equation

88
,
now

\displaystyle r_{p}^{-1}(\lambda r_{p}(\sigma\sqrt{2t}))^{2}

(103)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sigma^{2}\ln(\lambda r_{p}(\sigma\sqrt{2t})\cdot(2\pi\sigma^{2})^{d/2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sigma^{2}\ln\left(\dfrac{\lambda}{(2\pi\sigma^{2})^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\dfrac{2t\sigma^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\cdot(2\pi\sigma^{2})^{d/2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sigma^{2}(\ln\lambda-t)=2t\sigma^{2}-2\sigma^{2}\ln\lambda.

By plugging this equation into the proof of
Theorem

5
, we prove
Theorem

8
.
∎

In practice, DSRS with standard Gaussian and truncated standard Gaussian as smoothing distributions gives marginal improvements over Neyman-Pearson-based certification.
This is because, for standard Gaussian distribution, the noise magnitude is particularly concentrated on a thin shell as reflected by the green curve in
Figure

4
.
As a result, the truncated standard Gaussian as
{\mathcal{Q}}
either has a tiny density overlap with
{\mathcal{P}}
or provides highly similar information (i.e.,
Q_{A}\approx P_{A}
).
In either case,
{\mathcal{Q}}
provides little additional information.
Therefore, in practice, we do not use standard Gaussian and truncated standard Gaussian as
{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}
, which is also justified by
Theorem

2
, though DSRS can provide certification for this setting.

H.2
Certification with Generalized Gaussian with Different Variances

,

, and

(see definition in

).

for notation description.
